DREAM STREET SHUFFLE: the story, for review
Exported 7 November 2026 (rebuilt after the day's fixes and the night run) from the game's Twine (Harlowe 3) source. Code, styling, drawings, 3D scenes and sound have been removed; what is left is every passage of story text, in source order.

How to read it:
- "=== Name [tags]" starts a passage. Tags such as venue-french or dream say where it sits.
- [[Text|Target]] is a link the player clicks; [[Target]] links by name.
- Harlowe macros are kept because they carry the logic: (set:) changes a variable, (if:)[...] (else:)[...] show text only when a condition holds, (link: "Text")[...] reveals text on click, (display: "X") inserts another passage, (go-to:) jumps. $variables persist through the night; $sobriety and $confidence (shown as MORALE) are the two meters, 0 to 100.
- Text between ⟦DRAFT⟧ and ⟦/DRAFT⟧ is a placeholder drafted by an assistant, shown in pink in the game for the author to rewrite. Do not judge it as the author's writing.
- Removing the code also removed which elements are hidden. Some links that look live here sit in hidden or testing-only elements and never reach players, and players never see passage names. Check the .twee before calling a link a bug.
- The appendix at the end holds text that lives inside the game's code: the inner voices you hear when you click objects in each room (THE PRIME, THE OLDBOY, THE THIRST), and the trails of lines that lead from room to room to each dream world's pocket key. Some voice lines may also be drafts.

=== Approach Shana [venue-trishas]
(display: "Trisha Strip SVG")

Her lovers are always speechless, beautiful women; her male companions keep a tonsured silence.

She doesn’t look up but knows you’re there because she has read the Angelic Keys of Edward Kelly, and has such a talent for practical magick. Drinks land on the table, all whiskies, one for each of her five companions and somehow one more for you. She is yet to look up.

‘Give it here,’ she says.

[[Hand over the manuscript|Shana Reads]]
[[Not yet.|Trisha's]]

=== Approach the novelist [venue-french char-novelist]
(display: "French Rule SVG h60")
(display: "French Reentry")

Between the ground you’ve taken and his position falls an amber monolith, through the window onto Dean Street; a thick and molten bar on the gnarly floor, light and apart. If you lingered in it you’d become a fossil, like the filoplume of a silent dinosaur.

You once told him that he should stick to poems.

What have you got to lose?

[[Nothing|The novelist]]
(link: "Everything")[(set: $confidence to ($statLoss: $confidence, 6))(go-to: "The novelist")]

(display: "French Rule SVG h24")

=== At the Corner of Dean Street and Greek Street [outdoor char-red]
A man, not much past twenty, stands under a ginger light. At first you think he must be a dilly boy. He wears a beret and paints his nails; he is epicene and crinoline, as if he might snap at any moment.

Then you clock him for a poet that you know. A poet called RED.

Like a tiger that moves in the Taiga, whose footprints fill as soon as they are made, what RED leaves of himself on the street is trackless at your shutter-speed; in proximity he gives an impression of the weightless bergs of ash he calves into the puddle at his feet, which, even as they should extinguish are, by some feeling wind, awakened to a transitory brightness.

You stand nearby for a while; he is somewhere elsewhere, vast.

[[Speak to him|You speak to the poet]]

=== Beaten [venue-cellar char-john char-salvu]
(set: $wasBeaten to true)
(if: $crossed is "john")[(set: $savedBy to "nobody")](else-if: $crossed is "ashton")[(set: $savedBy to "ashton")](else:)[(set: $savedBy to "john")]\
(if: $savedBy is "john")[
‘I don’t talk to liars,’ he roars, when a voice hollers from the staircase:

‘SALVU! Leave it! He’s alright.’ It’s JOHN ST. JOHN. ‘I know him. I don’t like the bloke but he’s sound.’

ST. JOHN’S face is legible only to you, who once knew him so well. He’s both amused at your straits and miffed that it falls upon him once again to rescue you.

(if: $metRed is true and $crossed is not "red")[He leans close as he pulls you to your feet: ‘RED told COPPER you had money.’ You’re lucky, you reflect, that COPPER could work out you don’t.]
(if: $crossed is "red")[He heard you rat on RED and it seems you impressed him.]

‘Well,’ says JOHN. ‘That went better than anyone expected.’

](else-if: $savedBy is "ashton")[(set: $holdsAshtonName to true)
COPPER, fretful lion, shakes his jowls. ASHTON whispers something in his ear that seems to overfill him presto with a newly minted calm; such dreadful service must her lips have paid.

‘Maybe I rang customs,’ says ASHTON, the grinning tease. If she meant to confess then this is how she’d do it; but you can’t be sure what she means; you can’t even be sure that she knows what she means.

[[Up the stairs|The dark pass]]
](else:)[(set: $confidence to ($statLoss: $confidence, 12))
COPPER roars in the stairwell and shakes his jowls. His strength is in the singularity of revealed violence. Like all Godfathers he can stand alone; the firm’s a means to hurry things along or shift the blame. Just as the debt stands, so, at length, do you.

=== Cecil Court [venue-cecilcourt]
(display: "Cecil Rule SVG")
Cecil Court
A light’s on late in Cecil Court, leaching its scrumpy amber into the fog that settles between the shopfronts. This is the Booksellers’ street. In dimmed windows you glimpse the things you covet: first impressions with uncorrected misprints, presentation copies and pirated stuff.

On your way to the amber light you pass by Watkins, longtime tenants at numbers 19-21; hoary purveyor of the occult and the esoteric.

(link: "⟡ LORE: Watkins ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Watkins")(display: "Lore Read")
John Watkins, an ecumenical occultist bookseller and associate of Madame Blavatsky, opened the shop in 1901. His son Geoffrey ran it after him and, as of 1973, has only just retired; though he still haunts the place, like his father does.

Cecil Court is known in the trade as Death Row.

The origin of that name is far from certain, but it is, probably, traceable at least to Dr. Johnson’s The Rambler, wherein he remarks on the tendency of the alleyway, for such it was then, to gather, after rain, though it was some way from the river, the corpses of cats and wild geese.

Crowley came here and Yeats, Waite and Mathers. Mozart sold tickets to his shows from the shop at number nine. It is a dangerous bookshop.
]
(if: ($harpRouteOn is true) and ($returnedPage is true))[[[Go on to the back of the Harp|Harp Alley Approach]]
[[Back to the street|Dean Street]]](else:)[[[Look in|Watkins]]]

=== The Harp's Alley [outdoor]
(set: $harpMet to true)\

=== Davy Merkin [venue-colony char-davy]
(unless: $metDavy is a boolean)[(set: $metDavy to false)]\
(set: $metDavy to true)\

(display: "Colony Reentry")
He’s wearing a suit that was probably clean this morning, which means he’s been home and changed, but now it’s beginning to crumple in sympathy with his mood.

(set: _bagBook to ((($bookAt is not a string) or ($bookAt is "")) and ($bookSold is not true) and (($bookLent is not a string) or ($bookLent is ""))))(if: _bagBook)[‘Not interested,’ he says.‘Not interested.’ ]His audience, in parallel, is you, all skittish-pissed and needy, and the whole roiling gross of humanity, from Adam Kadmon’s day to his own, wherein a man still seeks a viable interlocutor.

The accent is Anglo-Antipodean; too clipped and politic to be functional down under, yet here in the imperial core, it strays audibly at its burnt edges into an outback.

‘I read last night in Oxford. Magnificent, actually. Met a girl; pretty woman, working on a D.Phil. at Magdalen, on Shakespeare and Co. She said she’d meet me today at the Pillars.’

He drinks so you drink and landing his glass he seems to clock you for the first time again, as he draws the whole, traversable world into his intimate sphere.

‘I got a bottle of claret and waited at the Pillars,’ he proceeds, spitting whispers. ‘She showed, sure enough. She showed and she didn’t touch a drop of it. So she left and I left and now here I am.’

You’re hushed.

You do. You used to wake up on the sofa in his mother’s house in Highbury, when you were both twenty-two. She was a patient woman and preternaturally kind, who let you sleep there with your whisky when you couldn’t go home.

London is all before you: Tyburn, Bloomsbury, Fitzrovia; St. Giles, at your feet; and Soho, which has dulled your soul and dressed you for the sun, in midnight’s cloak, throbs low and still, through its bruised capillaries.

You did not find the poem. Sometimes the night is its own work, and the dawn will come regardless.

=== Dawn [dawn-end piano-bed]

THE DAWN
(if: $bookSold is true)[// (print: $bookTitle) //Your bag is light. The book is on a desk on Frith Street, waiting for the office to open.](else-if: $bookLent is "lackland")[// (print: $bookTitle) //The book is on a shelf in LACKLAND’S office. He said he reads everything fast.](else-if: $bookAt is "foyles")[// (print: $bookTitle) //The book is on a pile outside Foyles, priced at pennies, and the dew is getting into it now.](else-if: ($bookAt is a string) and ($bookAt is not ""))[// (print: $bookTitle) //The book is still on a bar in Soho. Here you are, on Dean Street again, watching the light as it peels through its changes.

First cobalt; then the brassic crown that grows rich and gilded; then the antiseptic mint while the corolla rises and blooms through the lobes (frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital), the blue-blooded, hemorrhaging sky. Then the cool white quivering lip of the dawn’s bells and flowers when they’re trying to settle.

You have been awake for so long.

On.From the impossible door, LACKLAND emerges and cries, ‘Tomorrow! Tomorrow!’ as he crabwalks himself to a cab.

Perhaps things will be alright.](else:)[Yes, maybe tomorrow but till then you’ll lie with AOIFE and she’ll lie with you, and together you’ll raise a daughter.]

Better not tomorrow.

And the last weight peeled from the dawn is breathlike as a petal of the lily-of-the-valley which, caught on a thermal, is rising plumb into the childbearing sun, till the day settles up and it’s lost. You’ve forgotten the name of that flower.

=== Dido [dream pyre]
(unless: $visitedPyre is a boolean)[(set: $visitedPyre to false)]\
(set: $visitedPyre to true)\

On the pyre, DIDO is burning where she has burnt for 2,000 years.

The scent of her sour embers means something about lily of the valley.

(if: $hasMissingPage is false and $returnedPage is false)[‘Stay,’ she says. ‘My Carthage is meant for you. She is in her valley and will not return. Rest here and think no more about England.’]\
(else:)[‘You’re back,’ she says. ‘Carthage was always meant for you. What you took from the fire is yours; there is nothing else here. She is in her valley. Lie down beside me, now.’]

(link: "⟡ LORE: Her Dying Words ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Her Dying Words")(display: "Lore Read")Littora littoribus contraria, fluctibus undas
Imprecor: arma armis: pugnent ipsique nepotes:
Liue false Æneas, truest Dido dyes,
Sic sic iuuat ire sub umbras.

Shores against shores; waves against waves;
I curse them, arms to arms, may their children war.
Live false Aeneas, truest Dido dies,
So, so happily I go, under shades.Christopher Marlowe, Dido, Queen of Carthage (1594), after Virgil, Aeneid IV]
(link: "⟡ LORE: Christopher Marlowe ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Christopher Marlowe")(display: "Lore Read")
⟡ CHRISTOPHER MARLOWE ⟡

Editor’s note
Christopher (or ‘Kit’) Marlowe (1564-1593). A wild and capulet shoemaker’s son from Canterbury who went up improbably to Cambridge and became, according to RED, not only a spook but a double agent. Mad sorts believe he wrote Shakespeare’s plays. He did write Tamburlaine, Doctor Faustus and The Jew of Malta, and, with Thomas Nashe, Dido, Queen of Carthage, for the boy players of the Chapel Royal. Dido’s last words, here above, are from his play: Virgil’s Latin with one English line set among them. He was stabbed in the face in Deptford at twenty-nine, in a quarrel, according to rumour, over the bill at a brothel, and buried by drunken sextons in the dank and horrible ground. ‘Cut is the branch,’ as he wrote of his Faustus, ‘that might have grown full straight.’

]
(link: "⟡ LORE: Shelley’s Liver ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Shelley’s Liver")(display: "Lore Read")
⟡ SHELLEY’S LIVER ⟡

Editor’s note
Shelley drowned off Viareggio in July 1822, when his boat went down in a squall. Byron had christened the boat Don Juan and had DON JUAN painted on its mainsail; Shelley cut that out and called the boat Ariel instead. He was lost for ten days and washed up, so we believe, with either Keats or Hume or Sophocles folded open in the pocket of his jacket. The quarantine law said a body from the sea must be burned where it lay, so his friends built a pyre on the beach: Trelawny, Leigh Hunt, and Byron, who could not bear to watch and swam out to his yacht, sunburning himself in the Apollonian light. Trelawny pulled something that wasn’t burning from the fire; he thought it was Shelley’s heart but it was probably his liver. It fell into the possession of Mary, Shelley’s wife and the writer of Frankenstein and The Last Man, and Shelley’s most brilliant editor. She kept it for the rest of her life, wrapped, perhaps, in a copy of Adonais. Mary passed it on; officially, it was apparently buried with their son, Percy Florence, in 1889, though scholars of Shelley believe that one of them still guards it to this day, that it is passed down through the priest class of his cult. Just don’t ask who has it.

]

(link: "⟡ LORE: The Great Ham ⟡")[(set: _lore to "The Great Ham")(display: "Lore Read")
⟡ THE GREAT HAM ⟡

Editor’s note
When Dr. Johnson, the harmless drudge and titan nonpareil among London’s literati in the mid-eighteenth century, was at the height of his powers, he was called the Great Cham. (Cham being a variant of Khan, that is: supreme leader).

When Ian Hamilton occupied a similar position, two centuries later, nobody (I am sadly but reliably informed) bestowed on him the obvious sobriquet. Here I intend to fix that.

]

=== His round [venue-french char-john]
(display: "French Rule SVG h24")

(set: $sobriety to ($statLoss: $sobriety, 9))\
You nod; he orders two whisky-sodas.

Clocking first, his face, then your own in a pier glass behind him, you observe that you both look much older than you should, had you aged normally or healthily in the time since you stopped speaking.

His eyes shine like slate after cold rain. ‘How’s ASHTON GRANGER?’

You say you wouldn’t know. He’s gracious and decides to press no further.

‘You’re selling,’ he says. ‘I saw you buttering RED.’

JOHN ST. JOHN (if: $savedBy is not "john")[(for that is his name) ]is another poet. He hates RED. His rancour took seed in the days when they both used the same upstairs room in a Bloomsbury pub as their makeshift office.

(link: "⟡ LORE: On Red and John St. John ⟡")[(set: _lore to "On Red and John St. John")(display: "Lore Read")There is a cleaving-apart in English poetics, much as there is in philosophy, which derives from a difference of opinion about the French and the Germans. Where RED isn’t English, he is mostly French. He is Arthur Rimbaud on Charing Cross Road and his poems enact their dérive among the fragments of Mallarmé and Laforgue that Eliot shored up against the ruins in which the Great War left Europe.

In //Socialism: Utopian and Scientific// (1880), Friedrich Engels differentiates between socialists for whom socialism is mostly about acting to make the world better for those who live in it, who are often English, and those whose socialism is rooted in a scientific understanding of ‘will’, as an abstract, impersonal force, subject to the laws of dialectics, not Christian morality.

RED doesn’t know it, but he is of the scientific party. JOHN ST. JOHN, on the other hand, is an English poet, in the way that the Coverdale Bible is an English Bible.]

=== The Empty Glass [venue-french char-john]
(display: "French Rule SVG h24")
\
After these thoughts you drink and as you drink you notice that JOHN’S glass is drunk.

He drops it; it smashes.

‘Thank God, that was empty,’ he says, and stands.

You reach for your wallet and he waves you off.

‘Next time,’ he says, ‘it comes out in the wash.’

He’s gone and you know that you owe something now, to him and to here.

=== LINE 1

(set: $albaRevealed to true)
‘Forget the book,’ says RED, ‘it’s no way to live. Try this.’

As he turns from his ginger light he leans to kiss you and, as he does, you close your eyes. Inside their shuttered lids you read:

‘$alba1’

THE FIRST LINE OF THREE

You know this line, it begins Ezra Pound’s ‘Alba’. That used to be one of your favourite pieces of verse. ‘Alba’ means white, like the dawn; the only way out of this is through a morning song.

There are two more lines to find, if you want to escape. You’ll need to go beyond the pillars for the second. The third is buried somewhere in its night. Find them and perhaps you’ll make the dawn, for AOIFE and your daughter.

=== Maritime interlude [dream portal-music]
\
\
\
\

Turning onto Greek Street, you find yourself at sea and in a storm.

The broiling waters shred your ship till it’s leaf-tobacco, toss it like a stub.

A big hand splits the sky and a big voice booms:

NE. PLUS. ULTRA.

(link: "⟡ LORE: The Pillars of Hercules ⟡")[(set: _lore to "The Pillars of Hercules")(display: "Lore Read")
⟡ THE PILLARS OF HERCULES ⟡

The Pillars of Hercules stands on Greek Street, where Manette Street runs out under its arch towards Charing Cross Road. In the 1970s it was the office-at-large of Ian Hamilton’s //New Review//. The chaps at //Private Eye//, a satirical magazine, who must have disliked the //New Review// gang since some of them crossed paths at Oxford, kept watch from the windows of their own Soho offices, and noted how early each day the //New Review//'s editor made progress to his pub.

The Pillars, in myth, stand each side of the Strait of Gibraltar, Calpe on the European shore and Abyla on the African, set down by Hercules on his tenth labour. They marked the edge of the known world, and the warning carved on them, so the story goes, was NE PLUS ULTRA: Nothing Further Beyond. Charles V of Spain dropped the NE and put PLUS ULTRA on his coat of arms: Further Beyond.
]
Do you keep going?

\

(if: $inisToldOfPillars is true and $dreamKey is a string and $dreamKey is not "")[[[Seek the shore|The coast of Carthage]]](else:)[[[Seek the shore|The coast of Carthage]]](link: "⟡ LORE: Why Carthage ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Why Carthage")(display: "Lore Read")
⟡ WHY CARTHAGE ⟡

To pass the Pillars is to sail out of the Mediterranean into the open Atlantic, off every map the ancients drew. Turn back from the strait and the storm has only the inner sea to throw you into; a ship that will not go beyond is driven east along the African coast, to Carthage.

Aeneas washed up there the same way. Juno’s storm broke his fleet off Sicily and threw him on the Libyan shore, where Dido was building her city. He stayed a winter. Then the gods sent him on to Italy, and Dido built her pyre.
]]

=== No more
Centre Point is still behind you. It’s been there all night long.

The way that you conceive, now, of its height is more temporal than spatial and like time but not like space it remains impossible, merely notional; has a quality that space only attains when we imagine it infinitely.

One jumping from the top should fall forever and seem to fall as a ton of feathers falls in a perfect vacuum.

Standing here you can see London, Carthage, Liverpool and everything else.

INIS O’FLATTERLY clocks you, first with his eyes still downcast, then eventually as he looks up. ‘You’re a novelist and that wasn’t a question,’ he steams; with the gentle steaming of the kettle, not that of the railway.

=== O'Flatterly's quest [venue-cecilcourt char-inis threshold bookshop]
(set: _bagBook to ((($bookAt is not a string) or ($bookAt is "")) and ($bookSold is not true) and (($bookLent is not a string) or ($bookLent is ""))))‘Dear boy,’ he burrs, ‘I don’t know who you are, and don’t care who you are(if: _bagBook)[, and I don’t know that I don’t like what you’ve written; but do me a kindness, kid, and let’s just assume I won’t need it]. O’FLATTERLY coughs, disturbing the dust on the cover before him, agitating its thousand motes into flight, which hang over his desk like the clouds over Willesden Junction.

He slaps the manuscript like an arse and says: ‘Page 93 is missing. Find it for me!’You can swallow that; he’s neither publisher nor agent, and besides he’ll give you more than he could if he were. You’ll take the book and chance your arm elsewhere.

PAGE 93
INIS O’FLATTERLY is searching for a missing page; page 93 in his manuscript. You’ll need to leave Soho to find it. Return it to him.

(link: "⟡ LORE: Page 93 ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Page 93")(display: "Lore Read")
⟡ PAGE 93 ⟡

Editor’s note
93 is the number of Thelema, the religion Aleister Crowley proclaimed on returning from Cairo in 1904, after he was expelled from the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn. He claimed it was dictated to him by a spirit, Aiwass, on his honeymoon. The Greeks used their letters as numerals, and adding up the letters of a word gives its number; the practice is called isopsephy.ΘΕΛΗΜΑthelema, willΘ9Ε5Λ30Η8Μ40Α193ΑΓΑΠΗagape, loveΑ1Γ3Α1Π80Η893WILL and LOVE both add to 93, and the law of Thelema, the foundational creed of Crowleyism, joins them:Do what thou wilt shall be the whole of the Law.Love is the law, love under will.Aleister Crowley, The Book of the Law (1904), I.40 and I.57Thelemites greet each other with 93s. Watkins, in Cecil Court, sells his books.

]

=== Rescue the page [dream pyre]
You pause, for a whole long minute, and think of yourself, so as to become, Trelawny, salvaging what he thought was Shelley’s heart from the flaming corpse, and punch a fist into the flames to rescue the paper.

=== Return the page [venue-cecilcourt char-inis threshold bookshop]
You show him 93. O’FLATTERLY takes it, firmly and gently, then lays it into his papers, where it should be.‘I hear you, boy! I hear you! I can literally hear you!’ he burrs. ‘Ringing at the pitch of the vibrant scarab!’ Then looking, like a guilty dog, at your book, he adds: ‘But that is not a scarab.’(if: $pyramidNumber is true)[] He would shelve you, if he could, on his second best bed.

[[‘You don’t know the good that you’ve done’|O'Flatterly's Gift]]

=== O'Flatterly's Gift [venue-cecilcourt char-inis bookshop]
\
‘If I may, let me give you small thanks.’

From beneath the counter he produces a small glass vial.

‘This,’ he says, ‘is a slice of the liver of Percy Bysshe Shelley, the poet. It was cut from his body, as it burned on the beach at Viareggio, by his friend Trelawny, who mistook the liver for the heart. It would not burn, you understand. Swallow it when you feel yourself going out, and it will keep you up a while yet.’

=== O'Flatterly's Third Pillar [venue-cecilcourt char-inis bookshop]
(set: $inisToldOfPillars to true)\

INIS looks at the door and then at you.
‘There’s something they don’t say about the Pillars of Hercules,’ he begins, his thumbnails sitting on the counter’s lip. ‘The legends speak of two pillars only: one at Gibraltar, the other on the shores of the Maghreb. These are on either side of the pub’s front door.

‘But there were always three. Most people go a lifetime without ever seeing the third. I’ve been here so long, longer than a lifetime perhaps, that I have seen the third that stands between them.

‘Step into the third and you will find new worlds, such as were never dreamed between the Tyburn and the Fleet.

‘And know this too: the third blue post is hungry; be sure to have some offering to charm your way to the far side of the veil. You’ll find what you need as you wander; pay attention.

‘In fact there could be something in my shop that pays your way, though I wouldn’t look here first; you must explore. And if you find it standing, dear boy, go through.’

(display: "Key Doors Open Line")

=== After Cecil Court [outdoor street-night]
(set: $pendingLilyBreath to false)\
(set: $resumingFromCall to false)\

To return to Soho from Cecil Court is to cross the Styx; in some or other direction.

You wonder, limning Cambridge Circus, whether, to move from Cecil Court – that foxed God’s acre, full of books that lived and died – into the purgatorial womb of Soho, where books are promised, midwifed, scuppered, dreamed, is to move from death to life or life to death.

(link: "⟡ LORE: Ronnie Scott’s ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Ronnie Scott’s")(display: "Lore Read")
You hear four stories about Ronnie Scott’s and half of them are true.

1. My mother and father went one night in the eighties. No particular occasion and they didn’t know who was playing. They sat behind the piano; too close for anyone who really wanted to hear it. Nina Simone played that night.

2. Ronnie once gave a pianist a fulsome introduction: Where he’d played and who with; where he’d seen him play and with whom; and nobody came on stage. The band loops twice round the head and still nobody’s there. That’s the best I’ve ever heard him, said Ronnie. He was never heard of again.

3. You know about the jam they do upstairs? One night my friend’s friend was playing it; a physicist by trade and a brilliant jazz piano player.

Partway through his turn someone whispers in his ear: There’s a man in the stalls who will take a harmonica solo. The band turned the tune around then grew gentle to wait for him.

He comes in: it’s Stevie Wonder, it could be no other. I hear he’s a very kind man.

4. A man was paying for a scotch when the horn started soloing. He finished his drink, put on his coat, his hat and stepped out into the cold Soho rain to see a woman, returning just as the horn seemed to finish her solo.
]

=== Stay in Carthage [pyre]
You stay for an hour or forever.

Her pyre burns down while you’re close and all through the night, the body in its flames transforms, hanging the Lily-of-the-Valley on your nose that desires it; on the path of an asymptote, to something infinitesimally-not-her.

POLYBIUS: What do you mean by England? Why do you damn your country with its name?

[[Wake suddenly|The Interval]]

=== Talk to the Artists [venue-french]
(display: "French Rule SVG h60")
(display: "French Reentry")

You move towards their table, suspended, as it were, a thumb’s-width above the oak floor.

They do not look up. You’re about to bottle it when somebody speaks your name.

Among them is a face you recognise and he’s greeting you now. He is a Spaniard from El Rocío, in Andalusia, named BENITO DE LOS JUNCALES DEL OESTE. He painted you and ASHTON GRANGER once, when you were lovers, years ago in Cordoba.

My mediaeval knees lack health until they bend,
But in that woman, in that household where
Honour had lived so long, all lacking found.
[…]
No fox can foul the lair the badger swept.W. B. Yeats, ‘The Municipal Gallery Revisited’

You think of this, from Spenser and from Yeats, as you say hello.

[[Sit with him|The Spanish Artist]]
(display: "French Rule SVG h24")

You meet him, at the bar with a scotch in one hand and a fag in the other.

He’s getting a round in.

Him. ‘Pint of Guinness, double whisky, twice, and a gin and… no, you say it.’
You. ‘…and Bitter Lemon.’

You move to his table and place your drink on a little vacant patch among his papers.

You. ‘I like books…’

=== The Interval [interval-radio]
(if: $visitedInterval is true)[(go-to: "Dean Street")]\

(unless: $alba is an array)[(set: $alba to (a:))]
(unless: $haunts is an array)[(set: $haunts to (a:))]
(set: $visitedInterval to true)\
(set: $visitedCarthage to true)\
Salt on the lip. Cinder in the lashes. The pyre’s heat lifts from your face the way a hand lifts from your face — and you wake.

A flight of stairs, the kind you find leaving a theatre; red carpet worn thin as your world in its dead middle. Brass banister under your hand, still warm from other hands. Somewhere, a radio is playing spare: Love, O careless Love…

[[Continue|The Interval: the stairs]]

=== The Interval: the stairs [interval-radio]
(if: $intervalStairsSeen is true)[(go-to: "Dean Street")](set: $intervalStairsSeen to true)\

A woman passes; she is going up. Two steps down you freeze, stopped clock, right twice: you’d know that perfume anywhere. It’s LILY.
You catch a third line: ‘She lay beside me in the dawn.’ The words aren’t yours to keep yet.
And with it, her risen face:
That is not natural in an age like this,Being high and solitary and most sternW. B. Yeats, ‘No Second Troy’
(set: $confidence to ($statGain: $confidence, 12))\
(set: $sobriety to ($statGain: $sobriety, 10))\
She goes upstairs. You go down.
(link: "Turn and follow her")[You take the stairs by doubles but when you get there she’s gone. Lily-of-the-Valley is all that remains on the spendthrift draught.(if: $sawMemory3 is false)[(set: $sawMemory3 to true)In a certain memory she sits on your bed, dressing; she is in her underwear, which is filigree and black. Since then, and before it, life has rarely been so real. You’d burn Troy after Troy to see God’s face again.]

[[Down again.|Dean Street]]]

=== The Night Ahead [night-ahead]
(link: "alba-carry-1")[(unless: $alba contains $alba1)[(set: $alba to it + (a: $alba1))]](link: "alba-carry-2")[(unless: $alba contains $alba2)[(set: $alba to it + (a: $alba2))]](link: "alba-carry-3")[(unless: $alba contains $alba3)[(set: $alba to it + (a: $alba3))]]\

Autumn, 1973.

Soho, London.

You’ve written a book and you’ve a baby at home; tonight, somehow, you’ll make them both make sense.

The book took much longer to make than the little girl, and after an immediate, impromptu effort, you can’t take much credit for her, either.

She is born now and her mother, AOIFE, sits at home, with the babe in her arms.

Home’s cold, at home there is not enough food. If she’s told you once, she’s told you again that there’s not enough money coming in. You’re a good man so you’ve taken a job in a warehouse by the flat. But you’ll not make enough, not all year.

And so tonight you’ve gone back to Soho, with your book in your bag and a sense of the place as it was, ten years ago, when //no wife, no house or land still seemed quite natural.//

You’ll try, you suppose, the haunts you used to haunt and the people you knew. That’ll work: it’s a good book and God’s a good man.

[[Step into the night|Dean Street]]

=== Night Ahead: LILY [night-ahead]
\
(if: $lilyNamed is true)[(go-to: "LINE 1")]\
(set: $lilyNamed to true)\

You’ve been thinking about the form of the aubade, the morning poem, the song of wake-me-not-so-I-might-have-my-dream. You’ve been thinking about the first love of your life; LILY, from the rarest valley in Wales.

She’s why you’re here again, all told; you’d say that to yourself if you could stomach it.

[[On.|LINE 1]]

=== The Spanish Artist [venue-french]
(display: "French Rule SVG h60")
BENITO DE LOS JUNCALES DEL OESTE has a bottle of chablis, one-third-drunk, in his hand. He ushers you to an empty table, the one where the artists sat before they were too many.

He has the look of a man who is happy to watch the light change.

He takes an empty glass and pours for you.

‘So, you have seen the wheel,’ BENITO says, setting down chablis; ‘the turning wheel with eyes all round its rim. I see it too when I close my green eyes. I have tried to paint it; I fail to paint it. Do you think perhaps that you might meet their stare? The painter’s eyes look back through the same air with which they see him as he paints. Perhaps your words might look at it and somehow stay unseen.’(if: $ezekielVision is true)[]‘You’re selling something,’ he says. ‘It is making you unhappy. You cannot force someone to see what you have made.’

[[Listen|Benito's Hour]]

=== Benito's Hour [venue-french]
(display: "French Rule SVG h60")

‘In El Rocío, where I am from, there is each Whitsun a pilgrimage. A million pilgrims, on foot and on horses, go for three days through the marshes. They sing; they pray. When they reach the Virgin, they kneel. Next morning they go home again, to work and family. You might say nothing changes, but it does, mi amigo, it does.

‘In 1962 I painted a small thing – a girl asleep at a window. I was poor. I carried it from door to door in Madrid, to flog it. Nobody bought it. After many weeks I slid it under my bed.

‘Six years later, a friend came to help me move house. He saw it as we moved the bed from the bedroom. “This is mine,” he said. And it was; it had waited for him.

‘Everything you make already exists. You cannot change that. You make it; they might find it. That’s enough. It wants only to live.’

=== After the painter [outdoor]

(set: $resumingFromCall to false)\

The way you drew the painter is the way the world looks when you step out of the French. Things are faithful to your wild cartoon, until you blink. Then the place scrolls like a fruit machine and pays out Soho, all its stares and faces, its irresistible odds.

=== The Stranger at the French [venue-french char-john]
(display: "French Rule SVG h60")
(display: "French Reentry")

As you approach, someone else catches your eye.

He is skeletal, clad in leathers that serve for his skin. His suedehead veils the clean dome of a skull which, were its architecture less Roman, might seem vulnerable, but is, in its neoclassical form, more embolos than eggshell.

You know him as an old friend, from whom you are estranged over some personal business. He eyes you, raising his empty glass, then tilts his head, like a curious velociraptor, towards the bar. He is offering you a drink.

=== The coast of Carthage [carthage-cicadas carthage-shore]

[[·|Carthage shore]]

=== Carthage shore [carthage-cicadas carthage-shore]
(set: $visitedCarthage to true)\
You’ve left London. You are many miles and centuries far off. You’ve found safe harbour in Carthage, which is not like you remember it at all. These days it is swamped with Romans.

Last time you were here, you recall only SCIPIO AEMILIANUS, who was your friend and student and who weeping said:

‘A day will come when Ilium shall perish,
And Priam and his people shall be slain.’
Homer, Iliad VI, as Scipio spoke it over Carthage

(link: "⟡ LORE: Carthago Delenda Est ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Carthago Delenda Est")(display: "Lore Read")
Just north of Tunis and still given succour by the natural exposition of geographies, though piecemeal these days as if it had passed through a grinder, is Carthage harbour.

A levelled motte with all Africa as its bailey, the little islet lies flat as a landed coin: the downward face is Hannibal’s; that lodged against the sky is inscribed ROMA.

It is hard to know just when or where you are. Foam lingers at the sea’s brown lip unnaturally, endures in rabid pinnacles and domes, such as suggests a leaching of plastics. Strange insects spawn into ammonia clouds and chase you where the great galleys once berthed.

Overlooking the harbour is Flaubert’s house, or the house that we pretend was his, also in ruins. Stand by its jasmined gates of rotting iron and ask young SCIPIO just what he meant. He’ll tell you true, this is a falling hour.
]

He wept for Rome. You think about that still, sometimes, in England.

On the hill a queen burns upon a pyre.

And past the beach, a little bar: The Green Sea.

=== Aoife memory 2 [outdoor]

=== Turn to Copper [venue-cellar]
(set: $awaitingCopperPwd to true)\
\

‘Come in then,’ says COPPER.

You do as you’re told and he closes the door behind you.

‘Down them stairs.’

You don’t remember it being downstairs.

SALVU COPPER is a big man in a small body, or the other way round.

You both barrel into a cellar. It runs, you can be sure, at least beneath the Colony Room and probably one house further.

‘SALVU COPPER’ is a name you know and now you’re putting a face to it.

Since the Twins went down in ’68, his work’s been easier. He’s run his little fiefdom – pornography, strip clubs, hookers (the sorts of work that old fashioned East End crooks will swear blind they don’t touch) – very nicely, though lately he’s feeling the heat.

At the far end of the cellar, at a simple wooden table, sits ‘QUIET’ FRANKIE MUSCAT, who seems to you like nothing so much as a planet, the sort of man with moons. Yes, he is big, but his gravity comes, mostly, from his silence and a sense of power unused.

Opposite him (and her presence surprises you) is a poet you know, an American girl who comes from banking money, called ASHTON GRANGER. You agreed long ago to pretend not to notice each other, should you accidentally meet. She twirls her hair and scribbles without looking at the page, and gives QUIET FRANKIE a kind of angry come-hither look.
ASHTON is counting the stairs. She was JOHN’S before she was COPPER’S, and she was yours for a fortnight that neither of you mention now. You are not supposed to look at her, you look.

(link: "⟡ LORE: Infinite Riches ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Infinite Riches")(display: "Lore Read")

And, as their wealth increaseth, so incloseInfinite riches in a little room.But now how stands the wind?Barabas, in Christopher Marlowe, The Jew of Malta (c. 1590), Act I
]
‘So who are you?’ says COPPER. ‘And what do you imagine you’ve got to say for yourself?’

=== You speak to the poet [outdoor char-red]
(set: $metRed to true)\

‘RED!’ you say. He looks up, at first, then towards you without looking at you.

‘What’s the trouble, cock?’

‘I need to sell a book.’

‘Don’t look so merly. Things turn up.’

RED looks at you, phlegmatic as a leech. ‘There’s a word in your mouth,’ he says, ‘my professional advice is: don’t cough up.’(if: $easterGlyph is true)[]You don’t understand and smiling and then retreating, you strike, in the nifty, brisket air, a vein of his perfume, which you recognise, because you once loved a woman who wore the very same, as lily-of-the-valley.

=== Airport Pub [dream himalayan]
The Enlightenment has been in the Departure Lounge for as long as anyone can remember. Some claim they’ve found it shut but nobody believes them. It hums in the ear and the nose, settled as a fixture. There is jet fuel in the lager that gets you drunk.

The best drink is the drink-in-transit and this is the Wembley Stadium of such boozing. No point talking to strangers; anyone you meet will be half the world away come sun-up, somewhere. No, here we keep time to ourselves and watch for ‘Go to Gate’.

A man sits in a booth reading //Chariots of the Gods?//
(link: "⟡ LORE: Chariots of the Gods? ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Chariots of the Gods?")(display: "Lore Read")(display: "Lore Chariots of the Gods")]

He doesn’t introduce himself. His eyes are slate, and kind.

‘Half,’ he says, and writes on a napkin: Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata. ‘The rest is in the mountains, with me where the road ends.’

(if: $mantraHalf1 is false)[(set: $mantraHalf1 to true)

HALF A MANTRA
Datta. Dayadhvam. Damyata.

]

[[Final call|The Road to India]]

=== The Road to India [dream himalayan]
The road gives up three times in feint before it gives up for good. On the dashboard there is a photo of Dodi Fayed and Princess Diana. You are deep now, deep in the wild.

At Manali the bus stops one last time and since the road goes up, you walk.

[[Into the foothills|Foothills]]

=== Foothills [dream himalayan]
The thinning air in the foothills is wholly provisional; it wants you to leave.

You climb through villages and animals. A woman gives you tea with salt and butter. At last the pines give out and there is nobody else. Only you and the weather, which at this altitude gives you fair warning of its approach.

Prayer flags crosshatch the path with unlikely anchors. Sometimes you think you can see the curve of the earth in the dumb horizon’s giving blue.

You come to a fork. One path keeps climbing, the other curls back on the safe and imaginable valleys, and beyond them, home.

[[Keep climbing|The Mountain]]
[[Turn back|Himalayan Turn-Back]]

=== The Mountain [dream himalayan]
(set: $sobriety to ($statLoss: $sobriety, 12))\
(set: $confidence to ($statLoss: $confidence, 9))\

You crest another ridge (which must be but isn’t the last) and something is moving on the clean snow above you at a steady pace and not looking round.

SOBRIETY -12 | MORALE -9

[[Follow him|The Climb]]

=== The Cave [dream himalayan cave]
The cave is lucid and warm and feels somehow lived-in, not only by its present occupant but by a thousand generations of upright apes, most unknown and unknowable to Western science.

A butter lamp burns in a niche and near it, sitting in the lotus with a paperback, //Chariots of the Gods?// propped open on his knees, which were recently departed by a small black cat, is the slate-eyed man whom you met days ago in The Enlightenment. Near him, to his right, a pretty piebald kitten with a Jacobite goatee in black, offsetting her voluminous whiskers, curls in a nook.
(link: "⟡ LORE: Chariots of the Gods? ⟡")[(set: _lore to "Chariots of the Gods?")(display: "Lore Read")(display: "Lore Chariots of the Gods")]

‘Sorry about all the mess,’ he says. ‘Say it!’

=== Himalayan Turn-Back [outdoor]
(set: $tonightsWorld to "")\

=== Nazca Approach [dream nazca]
A bus stopped on a coastal road, sunning like a Guanay cormorant, and the Pacific on its wing growing pewter. Nobody else is getting off for Nazca.

It sets you down at a bluff cantina where a slip road cambers into the interior. Against the wall leans a propeller from a Cessna with its blade chipped at the tip; a man in a gaucho hat spills out of its shade.

‘Stay cool,’ he says. ‘Out there, there is no shadow.’

[[Out onto the pampa|Walking the Pampa]]

=== Walking the Pampa [dream nazca]
(set: $sobriety to ($statLoss: $sobriety, 6))\

He’s right, the Pampa’s thin and dry as parchment, as if you might tear through it if you breathe too heavy.

You’re walking a line where the stones have been cleared from the ground. It is, clearly, the work of human hands and yet the predictable ratio of its curve suggests the vision of some higher architect.

Some way to your right is a high ridge. From there you could see.

SOBRIETY -6

[[Climb the ridge|The Ridge]]
[[Turn back from the heat|Nazca Turn-Back]]

=== The Ridge [dream nazca]
(set: $sobriety to ($statLoss: $sobriety, 6))\

You climb it. Almost an hour of thirsty rock, then a flat top. From here, you can see.

The line you were walking is a wing. A beak needles the haze on the horizon; there’s a tail that splits into bars like a tuning fork. That is a hummingbird.

[[Down to the centre, where a car is waiting|Nazca Race]]

=== The Centre — Nazca [dream nazca centre]
You walk into the middle of the bird where its heart would be. The black car’s there, restoring its engine’s cool. (if: $nazcaRaceWon is true)[It got here after you.](else:)[It got here first.] Beside it is a cairn of sorts, a pile of screwball paper.

You recognise its driver from the shadow of the Cessna blade, he sits in the car’s open door, legs out and a notebook on his knee. He writes nothing, just tears the pages out and tosses them several feet onto the pile. They land and settle perfectly, every time.

‘That’s yours,’ he says, casting a paper ball that seemed as trackless as the others. You lift it off the cairn and prise it flat. The bird is on it, with you as a little black dot at its quavering heart.

=== Easter Island Shore [dream easter]
A fishing boat puts you ashore at night, its orange sail like a claggy paperback.

The beach is of basalt and the island above it has no trees.

Somebody has been down here recently: a spent cartridge litters the scree. Further inland there are forms; planked edifices, pivoted about the earth so they stand skywards; bald and spaced like the tines on a thumb piano.

[[Walk up the beach|Among the Moai]]

=== Among the Moai [dream easter]
These are the moai of Easter Island.

Between the fifth and the sixth a man with a rifle slung round his heft rides out of the dark on a squat, grey horse. The pipe he is smoking lolls from his lips, through the hole in his balaclava.

‘We took the beach at moonrise,’ he says. ‘For the governing council of the ancestors.’

‘Call me SUBCOMANDANTE FORTUNA.’

A thin sound needles into the edge of your hearing. It drones in till it swells and bevvies like a carnyx with its terrible harmonics.

=== Pyramid Mouth [dream pyramid pyramid-hum]
It is true – what they say – that you know the pyramids before you see them.

In fact you know them twice: once in their abstract form, which is, in the monuments at Giza, rendered in sui generis fidelity; and once historically, as the Pyramids at Giza, whose life among the Platonic forms has shaped, since you were a child, both your idea of what pyramids are in general and what these specific pyramids are here and now.

Perhaps some millennia hence, schoolchildren will learn ‘shard’ in the same way.

You climb the face until you reach a door.

[[Descend into the corridor|Descending Corridor]]

=== Descending Corridor [dream pyramid pyramid-chamber]
The passage cuts into the stone so precisely and at an angle so regularly declining that, if you didn’t know better, you would find it hard to believe it was cut with copper tools.

It ends in an unfinished chamber with a dark pit in the floor.

Inside it, though you cannot see him, somebody is humming. It is the Bard, AL HUBZ, laid out as if in his grave. He hums a scale, dancing among its intervals till he settles on the note he’s listening for. Then – louder, louder – hums till the chamber thrums like a glass harmonica.

A portal opens up in the far wall.

[[Into the dark, at a run|Pyramid Run]]

=== The Sanctum [dream sanctum]
\

The room is the shape of the long table that fills it. It is not dark, there are no shadows. The light, such as it is, draws from no source, is more a sort of background radiation, leftover, perhaps, from Genesis 1:3.

Your book is on the table; the GREAT HAM, hooded, monkish, looms above it.

‘It was all true,’ he says. ‘Who’d ever believe it?’

A kettle comes to the boil in a cold kitchen.

He opens the book at its end where, in your own hand but not by your own hand, is written this:

//You can go now. Everything is true.//

‘Take your own advice,’ he says and smiles.

(set: $sanctumDone to true)[[Go now|The Fetch]]

=== The Synthesis [dream synthesis]
\

You have everything you need: the mantra, the tracing, the glyph, the number and the vision. The three blue posts triangulate and plot the state of hyperstition where you stretch a singularity through several folds of Soho’s fabric, like a needle seen from above by a dancing angel.

(link: "Speak the mantra")[

MANTRA
(print: 1 of $mantra) · (print: 2 of $mantra) · (print: 3 of $mantra) · (print: 4 of $mantra) · (print: 5 of $mantra) · (print: 6 of $mantra)

(link: "Hold up the tracing")[

TRACING
The Hummingbird, from above

(link: "Remember the glyph")[

GLYPH
A glyph in Rongorongo

(link: "Hum the number")[

PROPORTION
A resonant number

(link: "Draw the wheel")[

VISION
The wheel-within-a-wheel

You draw the wheel in the air, outer, then inner. The eyes around its rim open and watch you to the thin end of the gesture.

The three blue posts transhift into the scalene and by Pythagoras’ metempsychosis, which is true, multiply fivefold, and lay themselves in the gullies of the pentagram you drew with gathered lilies over Soho.

{}

It is work to imagine Solomon’s Seal. Try this: two stars locked on a single point in space, round which they spin like dervishes.

Each is deformed by the other’s mass, such that, if you saw them from afar, you might think they were two dropped tears, weeping away from each other into infinite space and sharing a single eye at their grief’s fountain.

Closer though, you’d see that as each fills the distended blown-glass of its tear, it steals gas from the other, then collapses, and bellows that same gas to its twin bulb which stretches its own tear, returning.

Now imagine that seen from the earth through naked, crying eyes; the dazzle and the wink of such a star.

Your two great lovers in your permanent heavens. But distant, like gods who cannot intervene.

(set: $sawHexagram to true)\

=== Lore The Great Work [system]
\
\
⟡ THE GREAT WORK ⟡
Editor’s noteIn 1471 George Ripley, a canon of Bridlington in Yorkshire, dedicated to King Edward IV a poem on the making of the Stone. He called it The Compound of Alchymy and divided the Great Work into twelve gates, Calcination first, Projection last, each to be passed in its order. At the first gate he writes:At the fyrst Gate, now art thou in,Of the Phylosophers Castle where they dwell;Whych Castle ys round as any Bell:And Gates hath Eleven yet mo,One ys conquered, now to the second go.Tonight, like every night for those who live, you are an alchemist: ‘The Wheele of Elements thou canst turne about.’ Like any journeyman in the left-hand path you know that magic happens in the world and not within you, if it would be called magic at all. Soho is the little world tonight. Its haunts are seeded with twelve gates. Find them. They will not come in Ripley’s perfect order; a night so seldom does. But take them as they come to you and you might do such things…That ‘all the Castell thou holdyst at wyll,’Take fewer and the dawn will come regardless, though incompletely, like you.

=== Lore Chariots of the Gods [system]
Published in 1968, //Chariots of the Gods?// is a book by the Swiss author Erich von Däniken. It proposes that extraterrestrials visited Earth in antiquity and were worshipped as gods.Von Däniken interprets ancient monuments, artworks and religious accounts as evidence of these visits. He writes of the Egyptian pyramids, the Nazca Lines, the statues on Easter Island and the prophet Ezekiel’s vision of a heavenly chariot wheel.Scholars reject him as a fantasist, though quietly some will admit to enjoying his game.

=== JEFFREY’s tune [char-jeff venue-coach]
(if: ($dreamKey is "lighter") and ($jeffreyTune is not true) and (($keySlip is "seed") or ($keySlip is "Coach and Horses bar")))[
(set: $jeffreyTune to true)
JEFFREY palms the betting slip that was under his glass.
‘Light?’ he says, ‘I can’t read this.’
He knows the result but forgets which horse he backed.
You hold the lighter to him, over the paper which he pushes perilously close to combustion, and reading ‘Southgate’s Arkestra’ and knowing it didn’t place, slips the slip back under his glass and from his lips there is a tune he has been humming, louder now than the breath that it was under.
You listen till he stops.
Listen to the tune

=== BENITO’s drawing [venue-french]
(unless: $benitoDrawing is true)[(set: $benitoDrawing to true)You take the splint and pull. Blood begins to glaze his fingertip.
‘Give me that napkin.’
He swaddles his broken nail and presses it close.
You drink a sip or two and when, soon enough, the pain and blood subside and he can hold a tool again, he says, ‘Let me show you something.’
He draws four creatures, each with four faces and four wings. Turning the paper towards you, ‘Keep it,’ he says.
You put it in your notebook.]

=== BEVAN’s pigeon [outdoor]
(unless: $helpedBevan is true)[BEVAN GLYNDŴR opens his coat and shows you a pigeon in his pocket, with one wing broken.
‘I should guess it was hit by a car,’ he mumbles, looking down at it with such love and pity in his eyes that you could imagine, for a moment, that his own soul took the place of the pigeon’s, through sheer force of his empathetic will.
‘I can’t do it. Will you?’

=== Helping BEVAN [outdoor]
(unless: $helpedBevan is true)[(set: $helpedBevan to true)You take the bird from him.
Afterwards, he wraps it in a newspaper. You stay while he folds the sheets carefully.
‘You’re looking for LILY, I think?’
You ask how he knows her.
‘Wales is not a big place. Give or take a tide, it’s about as big as Wales. She stops by here, you know, on her way home; she’ll probably be passing by later.’
He puts the wrapped bird in the bin among the other things abandoned, fishes and chips, in their newspaper coats.
‘You might catch her,’ he says.]
[[Back outside the chippy|Approach Chinese Fish and Chips]]

- A bar | PRIME: The barman buffs his face out of the bar. | OLDBOY: This bar was in your dreams when you were young; you'll dream about it still when you've grown old.

## Ronnie Scott’s
- The Stage | PRIME: Just like I pictured it.
- A bar | THIRST: If you'd have practised harder you could have been a jazz musician; being an idle bastard has its mercies.

## the cellar
- QUIET FRANKIE | PRIME: That’s QUIET FRANKIE; you wonder how much he knows. | OLDBOY: They say he used to be a communist.
- A bulb | THIRST: The safest thing to do is to smash it and glass him. What's the worst that can happen: COPPER sees you off?

## the Coach and Horses
- A cow | PRIME: What do you call a cow with no legs? Ground beef! | OLDBOY: What do you call a cow with two legs? Lean beef! | THIRST: Ha! Don't milk it, son!
- JEFFREY BERNARD | PRIME: JEFFREY BERNARD is trying to remember your name. | OLDBOY: JEFFREY BERNARD is still here.
- JEFFREY BERNARD’S glass | PRIME: What's that beneath his glass? | OLDBOY: To meet your heroes looks like carelessness.
- Optics | PRIME: The optics are good. | OLDBOY: The optics aren’t good. | THIRST: The makings of a Long Island Iced Tea.

## Trisha’s
- Photographs | PRIME: They were all so exceptionally well dressed, the dead. | OLDBOY: Look at them all, still here!
- A door to the back | PRIME: You've never clocked that door before. | OLDBOY: NORMAN, titanic landlord of the Coach and Horses, once said there was a secret way out of Trisha's. | THIRST: Throw yourself at the wall, if you have to! Get yourself out quick!

## Lackland’s office
- A door at the back | THIRST: Go in, there's always someone there. Besides, you need to pass the time of day.
- Your book | PRIME: Not finished so much as abandoned. | OLDBOY: They say there's one in all of us. | THIRST: You never believed that bollocks. But in you?
- LACKLAND’S glass | THIRST: Which of us, shall we say, doth love it most, my Mr. LACKLAND?
- A dish | PRIME: Here’s looking at you, kid! A glass eye, green in the iris. You wonder why he keeps it. | OLDBOY: The second ashtray isn’t what you think it is; that green eye matches another, a blue eye somewhere else in the gathering dawn. Sooner or later you will learn what haunts it.
- Shelves | PRIME: Quite a lot of that new chap, Martin Amis. | OLDBOY: Saul Above, Saul Bellow.

## the chippy
- A range | THIRST: They should bring back whaling, as a sport.
- A menu | PRIME: COD, ROCK SALMON, SAVELOY, WALLY / CHOW MEIN, CHOP SUEY, FOO YUNG | OLDBOY: Forty-five new pence is nine shillings.
- A ticket | PRIME: This is one of many possible orders. | OLDBOY: Space and light and order. In this way heaven's like a late-night chip shop.

## O’Flatterly’s shop
- A glass case | PRIME: The prettier things are under glass and a small brass lock: a Kelmscott Chaucer, a Shakespeare quarto, or what claims to be a quarto, and on a saucer of its own a glass eye that looks straight ahead at nothing. It is not for sale tonight, at least not to you, not yet.

# TRAILS (each pocket key is found by following lines from room to room)

## The lighter trail, leading to Himalayas
1. The Colony Room, A piano: Some sheets of music rest on the piano; an old torch song in French, printed in Tunis, the work of some old pied-noir, no doubt. You'll find your own next step on the way to Carthage.
2. The Pillars of Hercules, A lattice window: On the sill beneath the window is a sheet of the same old song that was on the piano before. If you sang the chorus it would say: « Il y a un briquet sur le comptoir ! » ‘There is a lighter on the bar’, in French.
3. The French, A bar: Here. The brass lighter by the till. This is what the piano sent you for.

## The cocaine trail, leading to Nazca
1. the cellar, A bulb: This bulb is scratched with a single, straight, thin line. There the light looks whiter than the rest. It recalls the jukebox in Trisha's; you'll know it when you see it.
2. Trisha’s, A jukebox: From the jukebox, lines of thin wild mercury and their ghosts atop it. Too much for this small room; make a covert exit.
3. Trisha’s, A door to the back: At the threshold you clock something by your boot. That should be in South America. Wouldn't it be fun to take it home?

## The ticket trail, leading to Easter Island
1. O’Flatterly’s shop, A globe: See how half the world is water; fishes had no need for Noah’s ark. Go where the sea touches most close to Soho.
2. the chippy, A menu: There is, you think, some tedious irony in the menu's disorder. What did the globe in the bookshop show you? Order now and bear it in mind.
3. the chippy, A ticket: Fish is good for Friday, but this is the third step in the path you're walking. Saturday was hell; go to Easter Island.

## The slip trail, leading to the Pyramid
1. the cellar, A chesterfield: This is an old, fine Chesterfield. They say it used to belong to Howard Carter. Next, like him, you must go underground. Get drunk, until Osiris gathers you.
2. the Coach and Horses, The Gents: Someone etched a sigil on the door. A pyramid with an all-seeing eye. And what are all these letters?
3. the Coach and Horses, JEFFREY BERNARD’S glass: Here is the bedrock, an end of the road; gambling's the most squalid of the vices. But take the betting slip, you might as well; dream of the gold it promises.

## The eye trail, leading to Ezekiel
1. O’Flatterly’s shop, A glass case: In the cabinet, beneath the glass – the nictitating membrane, third eyelid – a glass eye looks back at you through your navel and browses the shelves. Its iris is blue, electric blue. Eventually you clock that the book it’s reading isn’t in this room at all but somewhere else, elsewhere in Soho. Look for other books then, a green eye to make a pair like Major Tom.
2. Lackland’s office, Shelves: In front of a bookshelf, you're always in the third person. Something in the room's eyeballing you. Look for it!
3. Lackland’s office, A dish: Immanentise the vision, pocket it. This one is a real angelic key.

# CALLBACKS (a later object answering an earlier one)
- 0 / now: Maybe LILY'S in this window, too.
- 2 / now: Another phone behind another bar. You always notice phones in pubs; they rarely bring good news.
- 2 / back: Another phone behind another bar. You always notice phones in pubs, you like how they promise news.
- 3 / now: Why must all these bars have telephones? A vision of hell: you can always be contacted.
- 4 / now: One day they'll make a phone that brings you good news.
- 5 / now: That's the same man you saw in a photo in The French, though there he was younger and with another woman.
- 6 / then: What a photo: Ronnie Scott, Les Paul and Fender Rhodes, together! Maybe they drank here.
- 7 / after: cu
- 7 / now: Downstairs again? Are you sure that's wise?
- 8 / now: Enough of this Les Misérables.
